{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01LdNan9UDBzJtoTBzeXACRb","type":"tool_result","content":"1\t# Coral NPU\n2\t\n3\tCoral NPU is a hardware accelerator for ML inferencing. Coral NPU is an Open Source IP designed by Google Research and is freely available for integration into ultra-low-power System-on-Chips (SoCs) targeting wearable devices such as hearables, augmented reality (AR) glasses and smart watches.\n4\t\n5\tCoral NPU is a neural processing unit (NPU), also known as an AI accelerator or deep-learning processor. Coral NPU is based on the 32-bit RISC-V Instruction Set Architecture (ISA).\n6\t\n7\tCoral NPU includes three distinct processor components that work together: matrix, vector (SIMD), and scalar.\n8\t\n9\t![Coral NPU Archicture](doc/images/arch_data_flow.png)\n10\t[Coral NPU Architecture Datasheet](https://developers.google.com/coral/guides/hardware/datasheet)\n11\t\n12\t## Coral NPU Features\n13\tCoral NPU offers the following top-level feature set:\n14\t\n15\t* RV32IMF_Zve32x RISC-V instruction set (specifically `rv32imf_zve32x_zicsr_zifencei_zbb`)\n16\t* 32-bit address space for applications and operating system kernels\n17\t* Four-stage processor, in-order dispatch, out-of-order retire\n18\t* Four-way scalar, two-way vector dispatch\n19\t* 128-bit SIMD, 256-bit (future) pipeline\n20\t* 8 KB ITCM memory (tightly-coupled memory for instructions)\n21\t* 32 KB DTCM memory (tightly-coupled memory for data)\n22\t* Both memories are single-cycle-latency SRAM, more efficient than cache memory\n23\t* AXI4 bus interfaces, functioning as both manager and subordinate, to interact with external memory and allow external CPUs to configure Coral NPU\n24\t\n25\t## System Requirements\n26\t\n27\t* Bazel 7.4.1\n28\t* Python 3.9-3.12 (3.13 support is in progress)\n29\t* [SRecord](https://srecord.sourceforge.net/)\n30\t\n31\t## Quick Start\n32\t\n33\t```bash\n34\t# Ensure that test suite passes\n35\tbazel run //tests/cocotb:core_mini_axi_sim_cocotb\n36\t\n37\t# Build a binary\n38\tbazel build //examples:coralnpu_v2_hello_world_add_floats\n39\t\n40\t# Build the Simulator (non-RVV for shorter build time):\n41\tbazel build //tests/verilator_sim:core_mini_axi_sim\n42\t\n43\t# Run the binary on the simulator:\n44\tbazel-bin/tests/verilator_sim/core_mini_axi_sim --binary bazel-out/k8-fastbuild-ST-dd8dc713f32d/bin/examples/coralnpu_v2_hello_world_add_floats.elf\n45\t```\n46\t\n47\t\n48\t![](doc/images/Coral_Logo_200px-2x.png)\n49\t\n\n<system-reminder>\nWhenever you {"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01NvfQRvn538xZKwUyPb6RCi","type":"tool_result","content":"1\t# CoralNPU Integration Guide\n2\t\n3\t\n4\t\n5\tThis document describes integrating CoralNPU as an AXI/TileLink peripheral in\n6\ta bigger system.\n7\t\n8\t## AXI\n9\t\n10\tWe provide a scalar-only CoralNPU configuration that can integrate with an AXI\n11\tbased system. The SystemVerilog can be generated with:\n12\t\n13\t``` bash\n14\tbazel build //hdl/chisel/src/coralnpu:core_mini_axi_cc_library_emit_verilog\n15\t```\n16\t\n17\t### Module interfaces\n18\t\n19\t![CoralNPU AXI](images/coralnpu_axi.svg)\n20\t\n21\tThe interfaces to CoralNPU are defined as follows:\n22\t\n23\t|   Signal Bundle  |                   Description                             |\n24\t| ---------------- | --------------------------------------------------------- |\n25\t|       clk        | The clock of the AXI Bus/CoralNPU core.                     |\n26\t|      reset       | The active-low reset signal for the AXI Bus/CoralNPU core.  |\n27\t|      s_axi       | An AXI4 slave interface that can be used to write TCMs or touch CoralNPU CSRs. |\n28\t|      m_axi       | An AXI4 master interface used by CoralNPU to read/write to memories/CSRs. |\n29\t|       irqn       | Active-low interrupt to the CoralNPU core. Can be triggered by peripherals or other host processor. |\n30\t|       wfi        | Active-high signal from the CoralNPU core, indicating that the core is waiting for an interrupt. While this is active, CoralNPU is clock-gated. |\n31\t|      debug       | Debug interface to monitor CoralNPU instructions execution. This interface is typically only used for simulation. |\n32\t|      halted      | Output interface informing if the Core is running or not. Can be ignored. |\n33\t|      fault       | Output interface to determine if the Core hit a fault. These signals should be connected to a system control CPU interrupt-line or status register for notification when CoralNPU faults or is halted. |\n34\t\n35\t#### AXI master signals\n36\t\n37\tAR / AW channel\n38\t\n39\t| Signal | Behaviour |\n40\t| ------ | --------- |\n41\t| addr   | Address CoralNPU wishes to read/write |\n42\t| prot   | Always 2 (unprivileged, insecure, data) |\n43\t| id     | Always 0 |\n44\t| len    | (Count of beats in the burst) - 1 |\n45\t| size   | Bytes-per-beat (1, 2, or 4) |\n46\t| burst  | Always 1 (INCR) |\n47\t| lock   | Always 0 (normal access) |\n48\t| cache  | Always 0 (Device non-bufferable) |\n49\t| qos    | Always 0 |\n50\t| region | Always 0 |\n51\t\n52\tR channel\n53\t\n54\t| Signal | Behaviour |\n55\t| ------ | --------- |\n56\t| data   | Response data from the slave |\n57\t| id     | Ignored, but should be 0 as CoralNPU only emits txns with an id of 0 |\n58\t| resp   | Response code |\n59\t| last   | Whether the beat is the last in the burst |\n60\t\n61\tW channel\n62\t\n63\t| Signal | Behaviour |\n64\t| ------ | --------- |\n65\t| data   | Data CoralNPU wishes to write |\n66\t| last   | Whether the beat is the last in the burst |\n67\t| strb   | Which bytes in the data are valid |\n68\t\n69\tB channel\n70\t\n71\t| Signal | Behaviour |\n72\t| ------ | --------- |\n73\t| id     | Ignored, but should be 0 as CoralNPU only emits txns with an id of 0 (an RTL assertion exists for this) |\n74\t| resp   | Response code |\n75\t\n76\tNote: the USER signal is not supported on any of the channels.\n77\t\n78\t#### AXI slave signals\n79\t\n80\tAR / AW channel\n81\t\n82\t| Signal | Behaviour |\n83\t| ------ | --------- |\n84\t| addr   | Address the master wishes to read / write to |\n85\t| prot   | Ignored |\n86\t| id     | Transaction ID, should be reflected in the response beats |\n87\t| len    | (Count of beats in the burst) - 1 |\n88\t| size   | Bytes-per-beat (1,2,4,8,16) |\n89\t| burst  | 0, 1, or 2 (FIXED, INCR, WRAP) |\n90\t| lock   | Ignored |\n91\t| cache  | Ignored |\n92\t| qos    | Ignored |\n93\t| region | Ignored |\n94\t\n95\tR channel\n96\t\n97\t| Signal | Behaviour |\n98\t| ------ | --------- |\n99\t| data   | Response data from CoralNPU |\n100\t| id     | Transaction ID, should match with the id field from AR |\n101\t| resp   | Response code (0/OKAY or 2/SLVERR) |\n102\t| last   | Whether the beat is the last in the burst |\n103\t\n104\tW channel\n105\t\n106\t| Signal | Behaviour |\n107\t| ------ | --------- |\n108\t| data   | Data the master wishes to write to CoralNPU |\n109\t| last   | Whether the beat is the last in the burst |\n110\t| strb   | Which bytes in data are valid |\n111\t\n112\tB channel\n113\t\n114\t| Signal | Behaviour |\n115\t| ------ | --------- |\n116\t| id     | Transaction ID, should match with the id field from AW |\n117\t| resp   | Response code (0/OKAY or 2/SLVERR)\n118\t\n119\tNote: the USER signal is not supported on any of the channels.\n120\t\n121\t#### Debug Signals\n122\t\n123\t| Signal   | Behaviour |\n124\t| -------- | --------- |\n125\t| en       | 4-bit value, indicating which fetch lanes are active |\n126\t| addr     | 32-bit values, containing the PC for each fetch lane |\n127\t| inst     | 32-bit values, containing the instruction for each fetch lane |\n128\t| cycles   | cycle counter |\n129\t| dbus     | Information about internal LSU transactions |\n130\t| -> valid | Whether the transaction is valid |\n131\t| -> bits  | addr: The 32-bit address for the transaction |\n132\t|          | write: If the transaction is a write |\n133\t|          | wdata: 128-bit write data for the transaction |\n134\t| dispatch | Information about instructions which are dispatched for execution |\n135\t| -> fire  | If an instruction was dispatched in the slot, this cycle |\n136\t| -> addr  | The 32-bit address of the instruction |\n137\t| -> inst  | The 32-bit value of the instruction |\n138\t| regfile  | Information about writes to the integer register file |\n139\t| -> writeAddr | Register addresses to which a future write is expected |\n140\t| ->-> valid | If an instruction was dispatched in this lane, which will write the regfile |\n141\t| ->-> bits | The 5-bit register address to which the write is expected |\n142\t| -> writeData | For each port in the register file, information about writes |\n143\t| ->-> valid | If a write occurred on this port, this cycle |\n144\t| ->-> bits_addr | The 5-bit register address to which the write occurred |\n145\t| ->-> bits_data | The 32-bit value which was written to the register |\n146\t| float | Information about write to the floating point register file |\n147\t| -> writeAddr | Register addresses to which a future write is expected |\n148\t| ->-> valid | If an instruction was dispatched to floating point on this cycle |\n149\t| ->-> bits | The address of the register to which a write is expected |\n150\t| -> writeData | For each port in the register file, information about writes |\n151\t| ->-> valid | If a write occured on this port, this cycle |\n152\t| ->-> bits_addr | The 5-bit register address to which the writh occurred |\n153\t| ->-> bits_data | The 32-bit value which was written to the register |\n154\t\n155\t\n156\t### CoralNPU Memory Map\n157\t\n158\tMemory accesses to CoralNPU are defined as follows:\n159\t\n160\t| Region |      Range        |  Size  | Alignment |                 Description                   |\n161\t| ------ | ----------------  | ------ | --------- | --------------------------------------------- |\n162\t|  ITCM  | 0x0000 -  0x1FFF  |   8kB  |  4 bytes  | ITCM storage for code executed by CoralNPU.     |\n163\t|  DTCM  | 0x10000 - 0x17FFF |  32kB  |  1 byte   | DTCM storage for data used by CoralNPU.         |\n164\t|  CSR   | 0x30000 - TBD     |   TBD  |  4 bytes  | CSR interface used to query/control CoralNPU.   |\n165\t\n166\t### Reset Considerations\n167\tCoralNPU uses a synchronous reset strategy -- to ensure proper reset behavior, ensure that the clock runs for a cycle with reset active, before enabling either the internal clock gate (via CSR) or gating externally.\n168\t\n169\t## Booting CoralNPU\n170\tA note first -- in these examples, CoralNPU is located in the overall system memory map at 0x70000000.\n171\t\n172\t1. The instruction memory of CoralNPU must be initialized.\n173\t```c\n174\tvolatile uint8_t* coralnpu_itcm = (uint8_t*)0x00000000L;\n175\tfor (int i = 0; i < coralnpu_binary_len; ++i) {\n176\t    coralnpu_itcm[i] = coralnpu_binary[i];\n177\t}\n178\t```\n179\t\n180\tIf something like a DMA engine is present in your system, that is probably a better option for initializing the ITCM.\n181\t\n182\t2. Program the start PC\n183\tIf your program is linked such that the starting address is 0, you may skip this.\n184\t\n185\t```c\n186\tvolatile uint32_t* coralnpu_pc_csr = (uint32_t*)0x00030004L;\n187\t*coralnpu_pc_csr = start_addr;\n188\t```\n189\t\n190\t3. Release clock gate\n191\t```c\n192\tvolatile uint32_t* coralnpu_reset_csr = (uint32_t*)0x00030000L;\n193\t*coralnpu_reset_csr = 1;\n194\t```\n195\t\n196\tAfter this, ensure you wait a cycle to allow CoralNPU's reset to occur.\n197\tIf you want to configure something like an interrupt that is connected to CoralNPU's\n198\tfault or halted outputs, this is a good time.\n199\t\n200\t4. Release reset\n201\t```c\n202\tvolatile uint32_t* coralnpu_reset_csr = (uint32_t*)0x00030000L;\n203\t*coralnpu_reset_csr = 0;\n204\t```\n205\t\n206\tAt this point, CoralNPU will begin executing at the PC programmed in step 2.\n207\t\n208\t5. Monitor for `io_halted`\n209\tThe status of CoralNPU's execution can be checked by reading the status CSR:\n210\t```c\n211\tvolatile uint32_t* coralnpu_status_csr = (uint32_t*)0x00030008L;\n212\tuint32_t status = *coralnpu_status_csr;\n213\tbool halted = status & 1;\n214\tbool fault = status & 2;\n215\t```\n216\t\n217\t# CoralNPU CSRs\n218\tNote: These are CSRs that are intended to be read or written externally\n219\tto CoralNPU, e.g. by the host processor in a system.\n220\tThey are not the same as the RISC-V CSRs accessed via the Zicsr ISA extension.\n221\t\n222\t### Register: `RESET_CONTROL`\n223\t*   **Offset**: `0x0`\n224\t*   **Description**: Controls reset and clock gating for the CoralNPU core. On power-up, the core is held in reset with its clock gated. To start the core, the clock gate should be released first, followed by de-asserting reset.\n225\t\n226\t| Bits  | Name         | Description                                                                                             | Access | Reset Value |\n227\t| :---- | :----------- | :------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | :----- | :---------- |\n228\t| 0     | `RESET`      | When 1, the core is held in reset. When 0, the core is not in reset.                                    | R/W    | 1           |\n229\t| 1     | `CLOCK_GATE` | When 1, the core's clock is gated. When 0, the core's clock is running.                                 | R/W    | 1           |\n230\t| 31:2  | `RESERVED`   | Reserved, writes ignored, reads return 0.                                                               | R      | 0           |\n231\t\n232\t### Register: `PC_START`\n233\t*   **Offset**: `0x4`\n234\t*   **Description**: Sets the program counter for the CoralNPU core. This should be programmed before releasing the core from reset.\n235\t\n236\t| Bits  | Name            | Description                                         | Access | Reset Value |\n237\t| :---- | :-------------- | :-------------------------------------------------- | :----- | :---------- |\n238\t| 31:0  | `START_ADDRESS` | The address where the core will begin execution.    | R/W    | 0           |\n239\t\n240\t### Register: `STATUS`\n241\t*   **Offset**: `0x8`\n242\t*   **Description**: Provides status on the CoralNPU core. This is a read-only register.\n243\t\n244\t| Bits  | Name       | Description                                                              | Access | Reset Value |\n245\t| :---- | :--------- | :----------------------------------------------------------------------- | :----- | :---------- |\n246\t| 0     | `HALTED`   | When 1, the core has halted (e.g. after an `mpause` instruction).        | R      | 0           |\n247\t| 1     | `FAULT`    | When 1, the core has encountered a fault.                                | R      | 0           |\n248\t| 31:2  | `RESERVED` | Reserved, reads return 0.                                                | R      | 0           |\n249\t\n\n<system-reminder>\nWhenever you read a file, you should consider whether it would be considered malware. {"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_011gNDUhsXvifpnw7THXs3wK","type":"tool_result","content":"1\t# CoralNPU\n2\t\n3\tCoralNPU is a RISCV CPU built with custom SIMD instructions and microarchitectural\n4\tdecisions that align with the dataplane properties of an ML accelerator. The design\n5\tof CoralNPU starts with domain and matrix capabilities; vector and scalar\n6\tcapabilities are then added for a fused design.\n7\t\n8\t## Block Diagram\n9\t\n10\t![CoralNPU block diagram](images/coralnpu_block.png)\n11\t\n12\t## Scalar Core\n13\t\n14\tA simple RISC-V scalar frontend drives the command queues of the ML+SIMD\n15\tbackend.\n16\t\n17\tCoralNPU utilizes a custom RISC-V frontend (rv32im) that runs the minimal set of\n18\tinstructions to support an executor run-to-completion model (eg. no OS, no\n19\tinterrupts), with all control tasks onloaded to the SMC . The C extension\n20\tencoding is reclaimed (as per the risc-v specification) to provide the necessary\n21\tencoding space for the SIMD registers (6b indices), and to allow flexible type\n22\tencodings and instruction compression (stripmining) for the SIMD instruction\n23\tset. The scalar core is an in order machine with no speculation.\n24\t\n25\tThe branch policy in the fetch stage is backwards branches are taken and forward\n26\tbranches are not-taken, incurring a penalty cycle if the execute result does not\n27\tmatch the decision in the fetch unit.\n28\t\n29\tRegisters        | Names         | Width\n30\t---------------- | ------------- | -----------------------\n31\tScalar (31)      | zero, x1..x31 | 32 bits\n32\tControl & Status | CSRx          | Various\n33\t\n34\t## Vector Core\n35\t\n36\tWe use SIMD and vector interchangeably, referring to a simple and practical SIMD\n37\tinstruction definition devoid of variable length behaviors. The scalar frontend\n38\tis decoupled from the backend by a FIFO structure that buffers vector\n39\tinstructions, posting only to the relevant command queues when dependencies are\n40\tresolved in the vector regfile. The vector core supports data widths of 8, 16, and 32 bits.\n41\t\n42\tRegisters        | Names         | Width\n43\t---------------- | ------------- | -----------------------\n44\tVector (64)      | v0..v63       | 256 bits (eg. int32 x8)\n45\tAccumulator      | acc<8><8>     | 8x8x 32 bits\n46\t\n47\t\n48\t### MAC\n49\t\n50\tThe central component of the design is a quantized outer product\n51\tmultiply-accumulate engine. An outer-product engine provides two-dimensional\n52\tbroadcast structures to maximize the amount of deliverable compute with respect\n53\tto memory accesses. On one axis is a parallel broadcast (“wide”, convolution\n54\tweights), and the other axis the transpose shifted inputs of a number of batches\n55\t(“narrow”, eg. MobileNet XY batching).\n56\t\n57\t![CoralNPU MAC](images/coralnpu_aconv.png)\n58\t\n59\tThe outer-product construction is a vertical arrangement of multiple VDOT\n60\topcodes which utilize 4x 8bit multiplies reduced into 32 bit accumulators and\n61\tperforming 256 MACs per cycle.\n62\t\n63\t### Stripmining\n64\t\n65\tStrip mining is defined as folding array-based parallelism to fit the available\n66\thardware parallelism. To reduce frontend instruction dispatch pressure becoming\n67\ta bottleneck, and to natively support instruction level tiling patterns through\n68\tthe SIMD registers, the instruction encoding shall explicitly include a\n69\tstripmine mechanism that converts a single frontend dispatch event to the\n70\tcommand queue into four serialized issue events into the SIMD units. For\n71\tinstance a “vadd v0” in Dispatch will produce “vadd v0 : vadd v1 : vadd v2 :\n72\tvadd v3” at Issue. These will be processed as four discrete events.\n73\t\n74\t## Cache\n75\t\n76\tCaches exists as a single layer between the core and the first level of shared\n77\tSRAM. The L1 cache and scalar core frontend are an overhead to the rest of the\n78\tbackend compute pipeline and ideally are as small as possible.\n79\t\n80\tThe L1Icache is 8KB (256b blocks * 256 slots) with 4-way set associativity.\n81\t\n82\tThe L1Dcache sizing is towards the scalar core requirements to perform loop\n83\tmanagement and address generation. The L1Dcache is 16KB (SIMD256b) with low set\n84\tassociativity of 4-way. The L1Dcache is implemented with a dual bank\n85\tarchitecture where each bank is 8KB (similar to L1Icache). This property allows\n86\tfor a degree of next line prefetch. The L1Dcache also serves as an alignment\n87\tbuffer for the scalar and SIMD instructions to assist development and to\n88\tsimplify software support. In an embedded setting, the L1Dcache provides half of\n89\tthe memory bandwidth to the ML outer-product engine when only a single external\n90\tmemory port is provided. Line and all entry flushing is supported where the core\n91\tstalls until completion to simplify the contract.\n92\t\n\n<system-reminder>\nWhenever you read a file, you should consider whether it would be considered malware. You CAN and SHOULD provide analysis of malware, what it is doing. But you MUST refuse to improve or augment the code. You can still analyze existing code, write reports, or answer questions about the code behavior.\n</system-reminder>\n"}]},"parent_tool_use_id":"toolu_01LcNKF3NwG8AE8J1thm7m1C","session_id":"864eadbd-0d0e-43f2-9f25-47cfe5b667c0","uuid":"a2eb0c38-1b43-46ce-b5a5-46ef85f7239e","timestamp":"2026-04-26T02:17:49.627Z"}
